\maketitle
\noindent This document contains all 50 \emph{Quadratic Inequalities} questions, each with a
fully worked solution and a TikZ sketch of the parabola. The roots are marked on the \(x\)-axis and the
solution set is highlighted in orange (open circles for strict \(<\)/\(>\), filled for \(\le\)/\(\ge\)).
Threshold lines are drawn for the double-inequality and modelling questions, and a family of parabolas
illustrates the discriminant (\(k\)/\(p\)) questions.

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 01\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-001)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 01 (ise4-001)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Find the set of values of \( x \) which satisfy \( x^2 - 5x + 6 > 0 \). Give your answer in set notation.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Factorise the quadratic.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 5x + 6 = (x - 2)(x - 3)
\end{gathered}\]
We look for two numbers that multiply to 6 and add to -5; these are -2 and -3. The roots are \( x = 2 \) and \( x = 3 \).

\textit{Step 2: Analyse the sign of the parabola.}
\[\begin{gathered}
a = 1 > 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; \text{parabola opens upward}
\end{gathered}\]
Because the leading coefficient is positive, the parabola is U-shaped. It is negative BETWEEN the roots and positive OUTSIDE the roots. We want it to be strictly positive.

\textit{Step 3: Read off the values of \( x \) for which the parabola is above the \( x \)-axis.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x < 2 \quad \text{or} \quad x > 3
\end{gathered}\]
The strict inequality \( > 0 \) excludes the roots themselves (where the curve equals 0).

\textit{Step 4: State the answer in set notation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\{ x : x < 2 \} \cup \{ x : x > 3 \}
\end{gathered}\]
A union of two disjoint open intervals.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle \{ x : x < 2 \} \cup \{ x : x > 3 \}\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 02\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-002)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 02 (ise4-002)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Find the set of values of \( x \) which satisfy \( x^2 - 7x + 12 < 0 \). Give your answer in interval notation.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Factorise the quadratic.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 7x + 12 = (x - 3)(x - 4)
\end{gathered}\]
Two numbers multiplying to 12 and summing to -7 are -3 and -4. Roots: \( x = 3, \; x = 4 \).

\textit{Step 2: Analyse the sign of the parabola.}
\[\begin{gathered}
a = 1 > 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; \text{U-shape; negative between the roots}
\end{gathered}\]
For \( a > 0 \) the quadratic is below the x-axis between the two roots. We want where the expression is strictly less than zero, so we take the region between the roots, excluding the endpoints.

\textit{Step 3: Write the solution as an open interval.}
\[\begin{gathered}
3 < x < 4
\end{gathered}\]
In interval notation this is \( (3, 4) \), with round brackets because the inequality is strict.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x \in (3, 4)\)
\end{answerbox}

\small The parabola sketched, with the roots marked and the solution set highlighted on the \(x\)-axis.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 03\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-003)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 03 (ise4-003)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( x^2 + 2x - 8 > 0 \). Give your answer in set notation.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + 2x - 8 = (x + 4)(x - 2)
\end{gathered}\]
Two numbers multiplying to -8 and summing to 2 are 4 and -2. Roots: \( x = -4, \; x = 2 \).

\textit{Step 2: Identify the shape of the parabola.}
\[\begin{gathered}
a = 1 > 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; \text{U-shape; positive outside the roots}
\end{gathered}\]
A positive leading coefficient means a U-shaped parabola: it is below the x-axis between the roots and above the x-axis to the left of the smaller root and to the right of the larger root.

\textit{Step 3: Read off the solution.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x < -4 \quad \text{or} \quad x > 2
\end{gathered}\]
Strict inequality, so the roots themselves are excluded.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle \{ x : x < -4 \} \cup \{ x : x > 2 \}\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 04\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-004)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 04 (ise4-004)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( x^2 - x - 12 < 0 \). Give your answer in interval notation.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - x - 12 = (x - 4)(x + 3)
\end{gathered}\]
Two numbers multiplying to -12 and summing to -1 are -4 and 3. Roots: \( x = -3, \; x = 4 \).

\textit{Step 2: Sign analysis.}
\[\begin{gathered}
a = 1 > 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; \text{quadratic is negative between the roots}
\end{gathered}\]
U-shaped parabola, so the expression is below the x-axis on the interval between the two roots.

\textit{Step 3: State the solution interval.}
\[\begin{gathered}
-3 < x < 4
\end{gathered}\]
Open interval because the inequality is strict.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x \in (-3, 4)\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 05\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-005)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 05 (ise4-005)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Find the set of values of \( x \) for which \( x^2 - 9 > 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Factorise using the difference of two squares.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 9 = (x - 3)(x + 3)
\end{gathered}\]
\( a^2 - b^2 = (a-b)(a+b) \). Roots are \( x = \pm 3 \).

\textit{Step 2: Identify the shape and sign regions.}
\[\begin{gathered}
a = 1 > 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; \text{U-shape; positive outside the roots}
\end{gathered}\]
A U-shape symmetric about the y-axis. The quadratic is positive where \( x \) is further from 0 than 3, and negative for \( -3 < x < 3 \).

\textit{Step 3: State the solution.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x < -3 \quad \text{or} \quad x > 3
\end{gathered}\]
Strict inequality, so \( x = \pm 3 \) are excluded.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x < -3\) or \(\displaystyle x > 3\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 06\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-006)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 06 (ise4-006)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( x^2 + 6x + 5 < 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + 6x + 5 = (x + 1)(x + 5)
\end{gathered}\]
Two numbers multiplying to 5 and summing to 6 are 1 and 5. Roots: \( x = -1, \; x = -5 \).

\textit{Step 2: Determine sign behaviour.}
\[\begin{gathered}
a = 1 > 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; \text{U-shape; negative between the roots}
\end{gathered}\]
The expression is below zero for \( x \) between \( -5 \) and \( -1 \).

\textit{Step 3: Write the solution.}
\[\begin{gathered}
-5 < x < -1
\end{gathered}\]
Both endpoints excluded because of the strict inequality.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle -5 < x < -1\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 07\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-007)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 07 (ise4-007)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( -x^2 + 4x + 5 > 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Multiply through by \( -1 \), reversing the inequality.}
\[\begin{gathered}
-x^2 + 4x + 5 > 0 \;\Longleftrightarrow\; x^2 - 4x - 5 < 0
\end{gathered}\]
Multiplying an inequality by a negative number flips the direction. This is the most reliable way to handle a negative leading coefficient --- convert it to a monic problem you already know how to solve.

\textit{Step 2: Factorise the monic form.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 4x - 5 = (x - 5)(x + 1)
\end{gathered}\]
Two numbers multiplying to -5 and summing to -4 are -5 and 1. Roots: \( x = -1, \; x = 5 \).

\textit{Step 3: Read off the solution from the monic inequality.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x - 5)(x + 1) < 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; -1 < x < 5
\end{gathered}\]
The monic parabola is U-shaped and lies below the x-axis between its roots.

\textit{Step 4: Sanity check using the original parabola.}
\[\begin{gathered}
a = -1 < 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; \text{n-shape; positive BETWEEN the roots}
\end{gathered}\]
For \( a < 0 \) the parabola opens downward, so the expression is positive between the roots --- opposite to the monic case. Both methods agree.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle -1 < x < 5\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 08\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-008)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 08 (ise4-008)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( -x^2 + 2x + 3 < 0 \). Give your answer in set notation.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Multiply through by \( -1 \) and flip the inequality.}
\[\begin{gathered}
-x^2 + 2x + 3 < 0 \;\Longleftrightarrow\; x^2 - 2x - 3 > 0
\end{gathered}\]
Reverse the inequality sign whenever you multiply or divide by a negative quantity. This is the most common error students make on these problems.

\textit{Step 2: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 2x - 3 = (x - 3)(x + 1)
\end{gathered}\]
Roots: \( x = -1, \; x = 3 \).

\textit{Step 3: Apply the U-shape rule to the monic form.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x - 3)(x + 1) > 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; x < -1 \;\text{or}\; x > 3
\end{gathered}\]
A positive-leading-coefficient parabola is positive outside its roots.

\textit{Step 4: Write in set notation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\{ x : x < -1 \} \cup \{ x : x > 3 \}
\end{gathered}\]
Union of the two intervals where the original expression is below zero.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle \{ x : x < -1 \} \cup \{ x : x > 3 \}\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 09\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-009)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 09 (ise4-009)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( 6 + x - x^2 > 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Rewrite in standard form and multiply through by \( -1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
-x^2 + x + 6 > 0 \;\Longleftrightarrow\; x^2 - x - 6 < 0
\end{gathered}\]
First arrange terms in descending powers of \( x \); then flip the inequality on multiplying by \( -1 \).

\textit{Step 2: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - x - 6 = (x - 3)(x + 2)
\end{gathered}\]
Roots: \( x = -2, \; x = 3 \).

\textit{Step 3: Use the U-shape rule.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x - 3)(x + 2) < 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; -2 < x < 3
\end{gathered}\]
Below zero between the roots for a U-shaped (\( a > 0 \)) parabola.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle -2 < x < 3\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 10\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-010)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 10 (ise4-010)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( 12 - 4x - x^2 < 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Rewrite in standard form and multiply through by \( -1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
-x^2 - 4x + 12 < 0 \;\Longleftrightarrow\; x^2 + 4x - 12 > 0
\end{gathered}\]
Flip the inequality when multiplying by \( -1 \). Now we have a monic problem.

\textit{Step 2: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + 4x - 12 = (x + 6)(x - 2)
\end{gathered}\]
Roots: \( x = -6, \; x = 2 \).

\textit{Step 3: Apply the U-shape rule.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x + 6)(x - 2) > 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; x < -6 \;\text{or}\; x > 2
\end{gathered}\]
Positive outside the roots for a U-shaped parabola.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x < -6\) or \(\displaystyle x > 2\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\begin{scope}

\small The parabola sketched, with the roots marked and the solution set highlighted on the \(x\)-axis.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 11\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-011)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 11 (ise4-011)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( x^2 - 6x + 8 \leq 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 6x + 8 = (x - 2)(x - 4)
\end{gathered}\]
Roots: \( x = 2, \; x = 4 \).

\textit{Step 2: Determine where the quadratic is non-positive.}
\[\begin{gathered}
a = 1 > 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; \text{negative between, zero at, the roots}
\end{gathered}\]
A U-shaped parabola sits below or on the x-axis on the closed interval between its roots.

\textit{Step 3: Include the endpoints because of \( \leq \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
2 \leq x \leq 4
\end{gathered}\]
At the roots the quadratic equals 0, which satisfies \( \leq 0 \). So the endpoints belong in the solution; use a closed interval \( [2, 4] \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle 2 \leq x \leq 4\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 12\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-012)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 12 (ise4-012)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( x^2 + x - 6 \geq 0 \). Give your answer in interval notation.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + x - 6 = (x + 3)(x - 2)
\end{gathered}\]
Roots: \( x = -3, \; x = 2 \).

\textit{Step 2: Apply the U-shape rule with inclusive endpoints.}
\[\begin{gathered}
a = 1 > 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; \text{non-negative outside / on the roots}
\end{gathered}\]
A U-shaped parabola is at or above the x-axis when \( x \leq \) smaller root or \( x \geq \) larger root. We include the endpoints because the inequality is non-strict.

\textit{Step 3: Write the solution as a union of closed intervals.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x \leq -3 \;\text{or}\; x \geq 2
\end{gathered}\]
In interval notation: \( (-\infty, -3] \cup [2, \infty) \). Square brackets denote inclusive endpoints; \( \pm \infty \) always takes a round bracket.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x \in (-\infty, -3] \cup [2, \infty)\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 13\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-013)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 13 (ise4-013)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( x^2 - 4 \leq 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Factorise using the difference of two squares.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 4 = (x - 2)(x + 2)
\end{gathered}\]
Roots: \( x = \pm 2 \).

\textit{Step 2: Determine the sign of the parabola.}
\[\begin{gathered}
a = 1 > 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; \text{U-shape; \(\leq 0\) between roots (inclusive)}
\end{gathered}\]
Includes endpoints because the quadratic equals 0 at \( x = \pm 2 \), which satisfies \( \leq 0 \).

\textit{Step 3: State the solution.}
\[\begin{gathered}
-2 \leq x \leq 2
\end{gathered}\]
Closed interval \( [-2, 2] \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle -2 \leq x \leq 2\)
\end{answerbox}

\small The parabola sketched, with the roots marked and the solution set highlighted on the \(x\)-axis.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 14\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-014)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 14 (ise4-014)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( x^2 - 8x + 15 \geq 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 8x + 15 = (x - 3)(x - 5)
\end{gathered}\]
Two numbers multiplying to 15 and summing to -8 are -3 and -5. Roots: \( x = 3, \; x = 5 \).

\textit{Step 2: Identify where the parabola is non-negative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
a = 1 > 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; \geq 0 \text{ on } (-\infty, 3] \cup [5, \infty)
\end{gathered}\]
A U-shaped parabola is at or above the x-axis outside (and at) its roots.

\textit{Step 3: State the solution.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x \leq 3 \;\text{or}\; x \geq 5
\end{gathered}\]
Inclusive endpoints because of \( \geq \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x \leq 3\) or \(\displaystyle x \geq 5\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 15\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-015)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 15 (ise4-015)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( x^2 - 6x + 9 \geq 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Recognise the perfect square.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 6x + 9 = (x - 3)^2
\end{gathered}\]
Discriminant \( = (-6)^2 - 4(1)(9) = 0 \), confirming a repeated root at \( x = 3 \).

\textit{Step 2: Use the property that any real square is non-negative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x - 3)^2 \geq 0 \;\text{ for all } x \in \mathbb{R}
\end{gathered}\]
A squared real number can never be negative; it equals zero exactly at \( x = 3 \) and is positive everywhere else.

\textit{Step 3: State the solution.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x \in \mathbb{R}
\end{gathered}\]
The inequality is satisfied for every real number, including \( x = 3 \) where the expression equals 0.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x \in \mathbb{R}\) (all real numbers)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 16\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-016)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 16 (ise4-016)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( x^2 - 4x + 4 > 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Recognise the perfect square.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 4x + 4 = (x - 2)^2
\end{gathered}\]
Repeated root at \( x = 2 \).

\textit{Step 2: Use the property of real squares.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x - 2)^2 \geq 0 \text{ for all } x, \text{ with equality only at } x = 2
\end{gathered}\]
The expression is strictly positive everywhere except at \( x = 2 \), where it is zero.

\textit{Step 3: Exclude the single point at which equality holds.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x \in \mathbb{R}, \; x \neq 2
\end{gathered}\]
Because the inequality is strict, we must exclude the value \( x = 2 \) which gives \( 0 \), not \( > 0 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x \in \mathbb{R}\), \(\displaystyle x \neq 2\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 17\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-017)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 17 (ise4-017)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( x^2 + 10x + 25 < 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Factorise as a perfect square.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + 10x + 25 = (x + 5)^2
\end{gathered}\]
Repeated root at \( x = -5 \).

\textit{Step 2: Apply the non-negativity of real squares.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x + 5)^2 \geq 0 \;\text{for all real } x
\end{gathered}\]
A real square is never negative. So \( (x + 5)^2 < 0 \) is impossible.

\textit{Step 3: State the conclusion.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{no real solutions}
\end{gathered}\]
The solution set is empty: there is no real \( x \) for which the expression is strictly less than zero.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad No real solutions (the solution set is empty)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 18\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-018)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 18 (ise4-018)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( x^2 + 4 > 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Compute the discriminant.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\Delta = 0^2 - 4(1)(4) = -16 < 0
\end{gathered}\]
The discriminant is negative, so the quadratic has no real roots and the parabola never crosses the x-axis.

\textit{Step 2: Determine the constant sign of the quadratic.}
\[\begin{gathered}
a = 1 > 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; x^2 + 4 > 0 \text{ for all } x
\end{gathered}\]
When \( \Delta < 0 \) the parabola lies entirely on one side of the x-axis, and its sign matches the sign of \( a \). Here \( a > 0 \), so the expression is strictly positive everywhere.

\textit{Step 3: State the solution.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x \in \mathbb{R}
\end{gathered}\]
The inequality holds for every real number.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x \in \mathbb{R}\) (all real numbers)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 19\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-019)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 19 (ise4-019)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( x^2 - 2x + 5 < 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Compute the discriminant.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\Delta = (-2)^2 - 4(1)(5) = 4 - 20 = -16 < 0
\end{gathered}\]
No real roots --- the parabola never crosses the x-axis.

\textit{Step 2: Complete the square to confirm the sign.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 2x + 5 = (x - 1)^2 + 4
\end{gathered}\]
Completing the square reveals that the minimum value of the expression is 4 (attained at \( x = 1 \)). The expression is always at least 4, hence strictly positive.

\textit{Step 3: Conclude.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 2x + 5 > 0 \;\text{for all } x \;\Longrightarrow\; \text{no real solutions}
\end{gathered}\]
Since the expression is always positive, it can never be less than 0. The solution set is empty.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad No real solutions (the solution set is empty)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 20\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-020)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 20 (ise4-020)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( -x^2 + 2x - 5 < 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Compute the discriminant.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\Delta = 2^2 - 4(-1)(-5) = 4 - 20 = -16 < 0
\end{gathered}\]
No real roots.

\textit{Step 2: Use the sign-of-\(a\) rule.}
\[\begin{gathered}
a = -1 < 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; -x^2 + 2x - 5 < 0 \text{ for all } x
\end{gathered}\]
When \( \Delta < 0 \) the parabola has constant sign equal to the sign of \( a \). Here \( a < 0 \), so the expression is strictly negative everywhere.

\textit{Step 3: State the solution.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x \in \mathbb{R}
\end{gathered}\]
The inequality is satisfied for every real \( x \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x \in \mathbb{R}\) (all real numbers)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 21\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-021)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 21 (ise4-021)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( x^2 + 3x \geq 10 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Rearrange to standard form (one side = 0).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + 3x - 10 \geq 0
\end{gathered}\]
You can only use the parabola sign-analysis once everything is on one side and compared to zero.

\textit{Step 2: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + 3x - 10 = (x + 5)(x - 2)
\end{gathered}\]
Two numbers multiplying to -10 and summing to 3 are 5 and -2. Roots: \( x = -5, \; x = 2 \).

\textit{Step 3: Apply the U-shape rule with inclusive endpoints.}
\[\begin{gathered}
a > 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; \geq 0 \text{ outside (and at) the roots}
\end{gathered}\]
A positive-leading-coefficient parabola is on or above the x-axis to the left of the smaller root and to the right of the larger root.

\textit{Step 4: State the answer.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x \leq -5 \;\text{or}\; x \geq 2
\end{gathered}\]
Inclusive endpoints because the inequality is non-strict.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x \leq -5\) or \(\displaystyle x \geq 2\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 22\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-022)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 22 (ise4-022)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( x^2 < 4x + 5 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Bring all terms to one side.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 4x - 5 < 0
\end{gathered}\]
Subtract \( 4x + 5 \) from both sides so the right-hand side is zero.

\textit{Step 2: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 4x - 5 = (x - 5)(x + 1)
\end{gathered}\]
Roots: \( x = -1, \; x = 5 \).

\textit{Step 3: Apply the U-shape rule.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x - 5)(x + 1) < 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; -1 < x < 5
\end{gathered}\]
Below zero between the roots for a U-shaped parabola.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle -1 < x < 5\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 23\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-023)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 23 (ise4-023)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( 2x^2 + 5 \geq 11x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Rearrange to standard form.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x^2 - 11x + 5 \geq 0
\end{gathered}\]
Subtract \( 11x \) from both sides.

\textit{Step 2: Factorise the non-monic quadratic.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x^2 - 11x + 5 = (2x - 1)(x - 5)
\end{gathered}\]
Look for factors of \( 2 \times 5 = 10 \) that sum to -11: these are -1 and -10. Split the middle term: \( 2x^2 - x - 10x + 5 = x(2x - 1) - 5(2x - 1) = (2x - 1)(x - 5) \). Roots: \( x = \tfrac{1}{2}, \; x = 5 \).

\textit{Step 3: Apply the U-shape rule.}
\[\begin{gathered}
a = 2 > 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; \geq 0 \text{ outside (and at) the roots}
\end{gathered}\]
Still U-shaped because the leading coefficient is positive --- only its steepness changes.

\textit{Step 4: State the solution.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x \leq \tfrac{1}{2} \;\text{or}\; x \geq 5
\end{gathered}\]
Inclusive endpoints.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x \leq \tfrac{1}{2}\) or \(\displaystyle x \geq 5\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 24\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-024)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 24 (ise4-024)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( x(x - 1) \leq 6 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Expand and bring to standard form.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - x \leq 6 \;\Longleftrightarrow\; x^2 - x - 6 \leq 0
\end{gathered}\]
Resist the temptation to read roots straight from \( x(x-1) = 6 \): the factor product equals 6, not 0, so this is not a difference-equals-zero situation. Expand first.

\textit{Step 2: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - x - 6 = (x - 3)(x + 2)
\end{gathered}\]
Roots: \( x = -2, \; x = 3 \).

\textit{Step 3: Apply the U-shape rule with inclusive endpoints.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x - 3)(x + 2) \leq 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; -2 \leq x \leq 3
\end{gathered}\]
Closed interval because of \( \leq \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle -2 \leq x \leq 3\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth \linewidth\else \width\fi}{!}{%
\begin{tikzpicture}[>={Stealth[length=2.2mm]},font=\footnotesize,line cap=round,line join=round]
\begin{scope}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 25\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-025)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 25 (ise4-025)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( 2x^2 + 5x - 3 < 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Factorise the non-monic quadratic.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x^2 + 5x - 3 = (2x - 1)(x + 3)
\end{gathered}\]
Two numbers multiplying to \( 2 \times (-3) = -6 \) and summing to 5 are 6 and -1. Split: \( 2x^2 + 6x - x - 3 = 2x(x + 3) - (x + 3) = (2x - 1)(x + 3) \). Roots: \( x = -3, \; x = \tfrac{1}{2} \).

\textit{Step 2: Apply the U-shape rule.}
\[\begin{gathered}
a = 2 > 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; < 0 \text{ between the roots}
\end{gathered}\]
Positive leading coefficient gives a U-shape; the expression dips below zero strictly between the roots.

\textit{Step 3: State the solution.}
\[\begin{gathered}
-3 < x < \tfrac{1}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Open interval \( (-3, \tfrac{1}{2}) \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle -3 < x < \tfrac{1}{2}\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 26\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-026)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 26 (ise4-026)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( 3x^2 - 7x + 2 \geq 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
3x^2 - 7x + 2 = (3x - 1)(x - 2)
\end{gathered}\]
Two numbers multiplying to \( 3 \times 2 = 6 \) and summing to -7 are -1 and -6. Split: \( 3x^2 - x - 6x + 2 = x(3x - 1) - 2(3x - 1) = (3x - 1)(x - 2) \). Roots: \( x = \tfrac{1}{3}, \; x = 2 \).

\textit{Step 2: Apply the U-shape rule with inclusive endpoints.}
\[\begin{gathered}
a = 3 > 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; \geq 0 \text{ outside (and at) the roots}
\end{gathered}\]
U-shape because the leading coefficient is positive.

\textit{Step 3: State the solution.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x \leq \tfrac{1}{3} \;\text{or}\; x \geq 2
\end{gathered}\]
Inclusive endpoints because of \( \geq \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x \leq \tfrac{1}{3}\) or \(\displaystyle x \geq 2\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 27\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-027)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 27 (ise4-027)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( 6x^2 - x - 1 \leq 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
6x^2 - x - 1 = (3x + 1)(2x - 1)
\end{gathered}\]
Two numbers multiplying to \( 6 \times (-1) = -6 \) and summing to -1 are -3 and 2. Split: \( 6x^2 - 3x + 2x - 1 = 3x(2x - 1) + (2x - 1) = (3x + 1)(2x - 1) \). Roots: \( x = -\tfrac{1}{3}, \; x = \tfrac{1}{2} \).

\textit{Step 2: Apply the U-shape rule.}
\[\begin{gathered}
a = 6 > 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; \leq 0 \text{ on the closed interval between the roots}
\end{gathered}\]
U-shape; below or on the x-axis from the smaller root to the larger one.

\textit{Step 3: State the solution.}
\[\begin{gathered}
-\tfrac{1}{3} \leq x \leq \tfrac{1}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Closed interval.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle -\tfrac{1}{3} \leq x \leq \tfrac{1}{2}\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 28\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-028)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 28 (ise4-028)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( 4x^2 - 12x + 9 > 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Spot the perfect square.}
\[\begin{gathered}
4x^2 - 12x + 9 = (2x - 3)^2
\end{gathered}\]
Discriminant \( = (-12)^2 - 4(4)(9) = 0 \), confirming a repeated root at \( x = \tfrac{3}{2} \).

\textit{Step 2: Apply the non-negativity of real squares.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(2x - 3)^2 \geq 0 \text{ for all } x, \text{ with equality only at } x = \tfrac{3}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Squares of real numbers cannot be negative, so the expression is at least 0 everywhere and exactly 0 only at \( x = \tfrac{3}{2} \).

\textit{Step 3: Exclude the point where equality holds.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x \in \mathbb{R}, \; x \neq \tfrac{3}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Because the inequality is strict, the single point at which the expression equals zero is excluded.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x \in \mathbb{R}\), \(\displaystyle x \neq \tfrac{3}{2}\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 29\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-029)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 29 (ise4-029)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve \( x^2 + 4x - 1 < 0 \). Give your answer in exact (surd) form.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Find the roots using the quadratic formula.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \frac{-4 \pm \sqrt{4^2 - 4(1)(-1)}}{2(1)} = \frac{-4 \pm \sqrt{20}}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
The expression does not factorise over the integers, so use \( x = \dfrac{-b \pm \sqrt{b^2 - 4ac}}{2a} \). Discriminant \( = 16 + 4 = 20 > 0 \), so there are two real roots.

\textit{Step 2: Simplify the surd.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\sqrt{20} = 2\sqrt{5} \;\Longrightarrow\; x = \frac{-4 \pm 2\sqrt{5}}{2} = -2 \pm \sqrt{5}
\end{gathered}\]
Roots: \( x = -2 - \sqrt{5} \) and \( x = -2 + \sqrt{5} \).

\textit{Step 3: Apply the U-shape rule.}
\[\begin{gathered}
a = 1 > 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; < 0 \text{ strictly between the roots}
\end{gathered}\]
For a U-shaped parabola the expression is negative on the open interval between the two roots.

\textit{Step 4: State the solution.}
\[\begin{gathered}
-2 - \sqrt{5} < x < -2 + \sqrt{5}
\end{gathered}\]
Open interval; the endpoints are excluded.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle -2 - \sqrt{5} < x < -2 + \sqrt{5}\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 30\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-030)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 30 (ise4-030)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve \( x^2 - 6x + 4 > 0 \). Give your answer in exact form.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Find the roots using the quadratic formula.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \frac{6 \pm \sqrt{36 - 16}}{2} = \frac{6 \pm \sqrt{20}}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Discriminant \( = 20 > 0 \) so there are two distinct real roots.

\textit{Step 2: Simplify the surd.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \frac{6 \pm 2\sqrt{5}}{2} = 3 \pm \sqrt{5}
\end{gathered}\]
Roots: \( x = 3 - \sqrt{5} \) and \( x = 3 + \sqrt{5} \).

\textit{Step 3: Apply the U-shape rule.}
\[\begin{gathered}
a = 1 > 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; > 0 \text{ strictly outside the roots}
\end{gathered}\]
A U-shape is above the x-axis to the left of the smaller root and to the right of the larger root.

\textit{Step 4: State the solution.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x < 3 - \sqrt{5} \;\text{or}\; x > 3 + \sqrt{5}
\end{gathered}\]
Strict inequality so the endpoints are excluded.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x < 3 - \sqrt{5}\) or \(\displaystyle x > 3 + \sqrt{5}\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 31\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-031)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 31 (ise4-031)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve \( 2x^2 - 4x - 1 \leq 0 \). Give your answer in exact form.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Apply the quadratic formula.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \frac{4 \pm \sqrt{16 - 4(2)(-1)}}{2(2)} = \frac{4 \pm \sqrt{24}}{4}
\end{gathered}\]
Discriminant \( = 16 + 8 = 24 > 0 \). Note the careful use of \( 2a = 4 \) in the denominator since \( a = 2 \).

\textit{Step 2: Simplify.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\sqrt{24} = 2\sqrt{6} \;\Longrightarrow\; x = \frac{4 \pm 2\sqrt{6}}{4} = \frac{2 \pm \sqrt{6}}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Cancel a common factor of 2 from numerator and denominator.

\textit{Step 3: Apply the U-shape rule with inclusive endpoints.}
\[\begin{gathered}
a = 2 > 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; \leq 0 \text{ on the closed interval between the roots}
\end{gathered}\]
U-shape; below or on the x-axis between (and at) its two roots.

\textit{Step 4: State the solution.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{2 - \sqrt{6}}{2} \leq x \leq \frac{2 + \sqrt{6}}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Closed interval; endpoints are included because the inequality is non-strict.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle \dfrac{2 - \sqrt{6}}{2} \leq x \leq \dfrac{2 + \sqrt{6}}{2}\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 32\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-032)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 32 (ise4-032)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve \( x^2 + 2x - 4 \geq 0 \). Give your answer in exact form.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Apply the quadratic formula.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \frac{-2 \pm \sqrt{4 - 4(1)(-4)}}{2} = \frac{-2 \pm \sqrt{20}}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Discriminant \( = 4 + 16 = 20 \).

\textit{Step 2: Simplify.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \frac{-2 \pm 2\sqrt{5}}{2} = -1 \pm \sqrt{5}
\end{gathered}\]
Cancel a factor of 2 in numerator and denominator.

\textit{Step 3: Apply the U-shape rule with inclusive endpoints.}
\[\begin{gathered}
a = 1 > 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; \geq 0 \text{ outside (and at) the roots}
\end{gathered}\]
Above or on the x-axis outside the two roots.

\textit{Step 4: State the solution.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x \leq -1 - \sqrt{5} \;\text{or}\; x \geq -1 + \sqrt{5}
\end{gathered}\]
Inclusive endpoints because of \( \geq \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x \leq -1 - \sqrt{5}\) or \(\displaystyle x \geq -1 + \sqrt{5}\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 33\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-033)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 33 (ise4-033)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Find the set of values of \( x \) that satisfy \( -2 < x^2 - 3 < 6 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Split into two simultaneous inequalities.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{(A)}\; x^2 - 3 > -2 \qquad \text{(B)}\; x^2 - 3 < 6
\end{gathered}\]
A double inequality is shorthand for the intersection of the two single inequalities --- both must hold simultaneously.

\textit{Step 2: Solve (A).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 > 1 \;\Longrightarrow\; x < -1 \;\text{or}\; x > 1
\end{gathered}\]
A common trap is to write \( x > 1 \) only and forget the negative branch. The inequality \( x^2 > 1 \) really says \( |x| > 1 \), which gives two intervals.

\textit{Step 3: Solve (B).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 < 9 \;\Longrightarrow\; -3 < x < 3
\end{gathered}\]
\( x^2 < 9 \) means \( |x| < 3 \), giving a single open interval.

\textit{Step 4: Intersect the two solution sets.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\bigl(x < -1 \;\text{or}\; x > 1\bigr) \;\cap\; (-3 < x < 3) \;=\; -3 < x < -1 \;\text{or}\; 1 < x < 3
\end{gathered}\]
Restrict each branch of (A) to the interval \( (-3, 3) \) given by (B). The left branch \( x < -1 \) becomes \( -3 < x < -1 \); the right branch \( x > 1 \) becomes \( 1 < x < 3 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle -3 < x < -1\) or \(\displaystyle 1 < x < 3\)
\end{answerbox}

\small The parabola sketched, with the roots marked and the solution set highlighted on the \(x\)-axis.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 34\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-034)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 34 (ise4-034)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Find the set of values of \( x \) that satisfy \( 0 \leq x^2 - 4 \leq 5 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Split the double inequality.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{(A)}\; x^2 - 4 \geq 0 \qquad \text{(B)}\; x^2 - 4 \leq 5
\end{gathered}\]
Both inequalities must hold simultaneously.

\textit{Step 2: Solve (A).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 \geq 4 \;\Longrightarrow\; x \leq -2 \;\text{or}\; x \geq 2
\end{gathered}\]
Equivalent to \( |x| \geq 2 \).

\textit{Step 3: Solve (B).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 \leq 9 \;\Longrightarrow\; -3 \leq x \leq 3
\end{gathered}\]
Equivalent to \( |x| \leq 3 \).

\textit{Step 4: Intersect.}
\[\begin{gathered}
-3 \leq x \leq -2 \;\text{or}\; 2 \leq x \leq 3
\end{gathered}\]
Take the parts of \( [-3, 3] \) that also satisfy \( |x| \geq 2 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle -3 \leq x \leq -2\) or \(\displaystyle 2 \leq x \leq 3\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 35\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-035)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 35 (ise4-035)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{Find the set of values of \( x \) that satisfy \( -1 < x^2 - 2x < 8 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Split into two inequalities.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{(A)}\; x^2 - 2x > -1 \qquad \text{(B)}\; x^2 - 2x < 8
\end{gathered}\]
Both must hold.

\textit{Step 2: Solve (A).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 2x + 1 > 0 \;\Longleftrightarrow\; (x - 1)^2 > 0
\end{gathered}\]
Rearrange and recognise the perfect square. A real square is strictly positive everywhere except at \( x = 1 \). So (A) holds for all \( x \neq 1 \).

\textit{Step 3: Solve (B).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 2x - 8 < 0 \;\Longleftrightarrow\; (x - 4)(x + 2) < 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; -2 < x < 4
\end{gathered}\]
Standard factor-and-sign analysis: U-shape, negative between the roots.

\textit{Step 4: Intersect.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(-2 < x < 4) \cap (x \neq 1) = -2 < x < 4, \; x \neq 1
\end{gathered}\]
Remove the single point \( x = 1 \) from the open interval \( (-2, 4) \), because at \( x = 1 \) inequality (A) fails (the left bound \( -1 \) is not strictly less than \( x^2 - 2x = -1 \)).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle -2 < x < 4\), \(\displaystyle x \neq 1\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 36\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-036)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 36 (ise4-036)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{Find the set of values of \( x \) that satisfy \( 3 \leq x^2 + 2x \leq 8 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Split the double inequality.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{(A)}\; x^2 + 2x \geq 3 \qquad \text{(B)}\; x^2 + 2x \leq 8
\end{gathered}\]
Both must hold simultaneously.

\textit{Step 2: Solve (A).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + 2x - 3 \geq 0 \;\Longleftrightarrow\; (x + 3)(x - 1) \geq 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; x \leq -3 \;\text{or}\; x \geq 1
\end{gathered}\]
U-shape, non-negative outside (and at) the roots.

\textit{Step 3: Solve (B).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + 2x - 8 \leq 0 \;\Longleftrightarrow\; (x + 4)(x - 2) \leq 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; -4 \leq x \leq 2
\end{gathered}\]
U-shape, non-positive between (and at) the roots.

\textit{Step 4: Intersect.}
\[\begin{gathered}
-4 \leq x \leq -3 \;\text{or}\; 1 \leq x \leq 2
\end{gathered}\]
Restrict each branch of (A) to \( [-4, 2] \): the branch \( x \leq -3 \) becomes \( [-4, -3] \); the branch \( x \geq 1 \) becomes \( [1, 2] \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle -4 \leq x \leq -3\) or \(\displaystyle 1 \leq x \leq 2\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 37\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-037)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 37 (ise4-037)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Find the set of values of \( x \) for which both \( x^2 - 4x + 3 \leq 0 \) and \( x > 1 \) hold.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Solve the quadratic inequality.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 4x + 3 = (x - 1)(x - 3) \leq 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; 1 \leq x \leq 3
\end{gathered}\]
U-shape, non-positive between (and at) the roots \( x = 1 \) and \( x = 3 \).

\textit{Step 2: Combine with the linear condition \( x > 1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
[1, 3] \cap (1, \infty) = (1, 3]
\end{gathered}\]
The intersection drops the left endpoint \( x = 1 \) (excluded by the strict linear inequality) but keeps \( x = 3 \) (included by the non-strict quadratic inequality).

\textit{Step 3: State the solution.}
\[\begin{gathered}
1 < x \leq 3
\end{gathered}\]
A half-open interval --- pay attention to mixed strictness.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle 1 < x \leq 3\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 38\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-038)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 38 (ise4-038)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Find the set of values of \( x \) for which both \( x^2 - 9 \geq 0 \) and \( 2x + 1 < 9 \) hold.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Solve the quadratic inequality.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 9 \geq 0 \;\Longleftrightarrow\; (x - 3)(x + 3) \geq 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; x \leq -3 \;\text{or}\; x \geq 3
\end{gathered}\]
U-shape, non-negative outside (and at) the roots \( x = \pm 3 \).

\textit{Step 2: Solve the linear inequality.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x + 1 < 9 \;\Longrightarrow\; x < 4
\end{gathered}\]
Subtract 1, divide by 2 (positive, no flip).

\textit{Step 3: Intersect the two solution sets.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\bigl(x \leq -3 \;\text{or}\; x \geq 3\bigr) \;\cap\; (x < 4)
\end{gathered}\]
Restrict each branch to \( x < 4 \).

\textit{Step 4: State the answer.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x \leq -3 \;\text{or}\; 3 \leq x < 4
\end{gathered}\]
The left branch \( x \leq -3 \) is already inside \( x < 4 \), so unchanged. The right branch \( x \geq 3 \) becomes the half-open interval \( [3, 4) \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x \leq -3\) or \(\displaystyle 3 \leq x < 4\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 39\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-039)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 39 (ise4-039)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Find the set of values of \( x \) for which both \( 2x^2 - 5x - 3 < 0 \) and \( x \geq 0 \) hold. Give your answer in interval notation.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Factorise the quadratic.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x^2 - 5x - 3 = (2x + 1)(x - 3)
\end{gathered}\]
Two numbers multiplying to \( 2 \times (-3) = -6 \) and summing to -5 are -6 and 1. Split: \( 2x^2 - 6x + x - 3 = 2x(x - 3) + (x - 3) = (2x + 1)(x - 3) \). Roots: \( x = -\tfrac{1}{2}, \; x = 3 \).

\textit{Step 2: Solve the quadratic inequality.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(2x + 1)(x - 3) < 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; -\tfrac{1}{2} < x < 3
\end{gathered}\]
U-shape (\( a = 2 > 0 \)), strictly negative between the roots.

\textit{Step 3: Intersect with \( x \geq 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
(-\tfrac{1}{2}, 3) \cap [0, \infty) = [0, 3)
\end{gathered}\]
The left endpoint becomes \( 0 \) (included via \( x \geq 0 \)); the right endpoint \( 3 \) is excluded (strict from the quadratic).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x \in [0, 3)\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 40\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-040)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 40 (ise4-040)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Find the set of values of \( x \) for which both \( x^2 + x - 6 > 0 \) and \( 3 - x \geq 0 \) hold.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Solve the quadratic inequality.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + x - 6 = (x + 3)(x - 2) > 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; x < -3 \;\text{or}\; x > 2
\end{gathered}\]
U-shape, strictly positive outside the roots.

\textit{Step 2: Solve the linear inequality.}
\[\begin{gathered}
3 - x \geq 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; x \leq 3
\end{gathered}\]
Rearrange --- add \( x \) to both sides, no flip needed.

\textit{Step 3: Intersect.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\bigl(x < -3 \;\text{or}\; x > 2\bigr) \;\cap\; (x \leq 3) \;=\; x < -3 \;\text{or}\; 2 < x \leq 3
\end{gathered}\]
The left branch is unchanged; the right branch \( x > 2 \) is capped at 3 (inclusive), giving \( (2, 3] \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x < -3\) or \(\displaystyle 2 < x \leq 3\)
\end{answerbox}

\small The parabola sketched, with the roots marked and the solution set highlighted on the \(x\)-axis.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 41\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-041)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 41 (ise4-041)}
\textit{Difficulty: Challenge \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Find the set of values of the constant \( k \) for which the inequality \( x^2 + kx + 9 > 0 \) holds for all real values of \( x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Identify the condition for "positive for all real \( x \)".}
\[\begin{gathered}
a = 1 > 0; \;\text{need parabola to never touch / cross the x-axis}
\end{gathered}\]
A U-shaped parabola is strictly above the x-axis everywhere if and only if it has no real roots. This translates to the discriminant being strictly negative.

\textit{Step 2: Set up the discriminant condition.}
\[\begin{gathered}
b^2 - 4ac < 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; k^2 - 4(1)(9) < 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; k^2 - 36 < 0
\end{gathered}\]
Here the variable in the original quadratic is \( x \), and \( k \) plays the role of \( b \). The discriminant in \( x \) depends on \( k \).

\textit{Step 3: Solve the resulting quadratic inequality in \( k \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
k^2 - 36 < 0 \;\Longleftrightarrow\; (k - 6)(k + 6) < 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; -6 < k < 6
\end{gathered}\]
A second quadratic inequality appears, now in the variable \( k \). Same technique as before: U-shape, negative between the roots.

\textit{Step 4: State the answer.}
\[\begin{gathered}
-6 < k < 6
\end{gathered}\]
For these values of \( k \) the original quadratic in \( x \) is positive for every real \( x \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle -6 < k < 6\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 42\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-042)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 42 (ise4-042)}
\textit{Difficulty: Challenge \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Find the set of values of \( k \) for which the equation \( 2x^2 + kx + 8 = 0 \) has no real roots.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: State the condition for no real roots.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\Delta < 0
\end{gathered}\]
A quadratic equation has no real roots exactly when its discriminant is strictly negative.

\textit{Step 2: Form the discriminant.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\Delta = k^2 - 4(2)(8) = k^2 - 64
\end{gathered}\]
With \( a = 2, b = k, c = 8 \).

\textit{Step 3: Solve \( \Delta < 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
k^2 - 64 < 0 \;\Longleftrightarrow\; (k - 8)(k + 8) < 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; -8 < k < 8
\end{gathered}\]
U-shape (in \( k \)); negative between the roots.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle -8 < k < 8\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 43\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-043)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 43 (ise4-043)}
\textit{Difficulty: Challenge \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{Find the set of values of \( p \) for which the equation \( x^2 + (p - 2)x + 4 = 0 \) has two distinct real roots.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: State the condition for two distinct real roots.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\Delta > 0
\end{gathered}\]
Two distinct real roots correspond to a strictly positive discriminant.

\textit{Step 2: Compute the discriminant.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\Delta = (p - 2)^2 - 4(1)(4) = (p - 2)^2 - 16
\end{gathered}\]
Here \( b = p - 2 \), so be careful to square the entire expression --- students often write \( p^2 - 4 \) by mistake.

\textit{Step 3: Solve \( (p - 2)^2 - 16 > 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
(p - 2)^2 > 16 \;\Longrightarrow\; |p - 2| > 4 \;\Longrightarrow\; p - 2 < -4 \;\text{or}\; p - 2 > 4
\end{gathered}\]
Take the square root after isolating the squared term. The square-root step introduces \( | \cdot | \) and hence two cases.

\textit{Step 4: Simplify each branch.}
\[\begin{gathered}
p < -2 \;\text{or}\; p > 6
\end{gathered}\]
Add 2 to both sides of each case.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle p < -2\) or \(\displaystyle p > 6\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 44\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-044)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 44 (ise4-044)}
\textit{Difficulty: Challenge \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( (x - 1)^2 - 9 \leq 0 \) without expanding the bracket.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Isolate the squared term.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x - 1)^2 \leq 9
\end{gathered}\]
Add 9 to both sides. Treat \( x - 1 \) as a single variable.

\textit{Step 2: Take square roots, introducing \( | \cdot | \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
|x - 1| \leq 3 \;\Longleftrightarrow\; -3 \leq x - 1 \leq 3
\end{gathered}\]
For any non-negative \( k \), \( y^2 \leq k^2 \) is equivalent to \( |y| \leq k \), which is equivalent to \( -k \leq y \leq k \).

\textit{Step 3: Solve the double inequality.}
\[\begin{gathered}
-2 \leq x \leq 4
\end{gathered}\]
Add 1 to each part of the chain.

\textit{Step 4: Sanity check by expanding.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x - 1)^2 - 9 = x^2 - 2x - 8 = (x - 4)(x + 2)
\end{gathered}\]
Roots \( x = -2 \) and \( x = 4 \) match --- the answer is correct.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle -2 \leq x \leq 4\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 45\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-045)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 45 (ise4-045)}
\textit{Difficulty: Challenge \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( (2x + 3)^2 > 25 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Translate the squared inequality to an absolute-value statement.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(2x + 3)^2 > 25 \;\Longleftrightarrow\; |2x + 3| > 5
\end{gathered}\]
For any \( k > 0 \), \( y^2 > k^2 \) is equivalent to \( |y| > k \).

\textit{Step 2: Break into two cases.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x + 3 < -5 \;\text{or}\; 2x + 3 > 5
\end{gathered}\]
\( |y| > 5 \) splits into \( y < -5 \) or \( y > 5 \) --- two disjoint pieces.

\textit{Step 3: Solve each linear inequality.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x < -8 \;\Rightarrow\; x < -4 \qquad \text{or} \qquad 2x > 2 \;\Rightarrow\; x > 1
\end{gathered}\]
Subtract 3 then divide by 2 (positive --- no flip).

\textit{Step 4: State the solution.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x < -4 \;\text{or}\; x > 1
\end{gathered}\]
Union of two open intervals.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x < -4\) or \(\displaystyle x > 1\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 46\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-046)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 46 (ise4-046)}
\textit{Difficulty: Challenge \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{Solve \( x^4 - 5x^2 + 4 \leq 0 \). [Hint: substitute \( u = x^2 \).]}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Substitute \( u = x^2 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
u^2 - 5u + 4 \leq 0
\end{gathered}\]
A quadratic in \( u \). Note that \( u = x^2 \geq 0 \) automatically, which we must remember when translating back.

\textit{Step 2: Factorise and solve in \( u \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
(u - 1)(u - 4) \leq 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; 1 \leq u \leq 4
\end{gathered}\]
U-shape; non-positive between (and at) the roots.

\textit{Step 3: Translate back to \( x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
1 \leq x^2 \leq 4
\end{gathered}\]
Replace \( u \) with \( x^2 \). This is now a double-bounded inequality in \( x^2 \).

\textit{Step 4: Solve the double inequality.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 \geq 1 \;\text{and}\; x^2 \leq 4
\end{gathered}\]
\( x^2 \geq 1 \Leftrightarrow |x| \geq 1 \); \( x^2 \leq 4 \Leftrightarrow |x| \leq 2 \). Together: \( 1 \leq |x| \leq 2 \).

\textit{Step 5: Convert the absolute-value statement to intervals.}
\[\begin{gathered}
-2 \leq x \leq -1 \;\text{or}\; 1 \leq x \leq 2
\end{gathered}\]
The condition \( 1 \leq |x| \leq 2 \) describes two symmetric closed intervals --- one negative, one positive.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle -2 \leq x \leq -1\) or \(\displaystyle 1 \leq x \leq 2\)
\end{answerbox}

\node[below,scale=0.66] at (5.6,1.933) {$1$};
\fill[red!75!black] (7,1.933) circle (2pt);
\node[below,scale=0.66] at (7,1.933) {$2$};
\node[blue!70!black,scale=0.66,anchor=west,inner sep=1pt] at (1.037,5.6) {$y=x^4-5x^2+4$};
\end{tikzpicture}
}
\end{center}
\small The parabola sketched, with the roots marked and the solution set highlighted on the \(x\)-axis.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 47\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-047)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 47 (ise4-047)}
\textit{Difficulty: Challenge \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{A particle is launched vertically and its height (in metres) above the ground after \( t \) seconds is given by \( h(t) = 20t - 5t^2 \). Find the values of \( t \) (with \( t \geq 0 \)) for which the particle is more than 15 metres above the ground.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Set up the inequality from the model.}
\[\begin{gathered}
20t - 5t^2 > 15
\end{gathered}\]
"More than 15 metres above the ground" means the height function strictly exceeds 15.

\textit{Step 2: Rearrange to standard form.}
\[\begin{gathered}
-5t^2 + 20t - 15 > 0 \;\Longleftrightarrow\; t^2 - 4t + 3 < 0
\end{gathered}\]
Divide both sides by \( -5 \) and flip the inequality. Dividing by a negative quantity always reverses the direction --- a very common mistake to watch for.

\textit{Step 3: Factorise and solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
t^2 - 4t + 3 = (t - 1)(t - 3) < 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; 1 < t < 3
\end{gathered}\]
U-shape, strictly negative between the roots.

\textit{Step 4: Check the physical domain.}
\[\begin{gathered}
1 < t < 3 \;\text{and}\; t \geq 0
\end{gathered}\]
The interval \( (1, 3) \) already lies inside \( t \geq 0 \), so no additional restriction is needed. Times before launch are not physically meaningful and are excluded by the model anyway.

\textit{Step 5: State the answer with units.}
\[\begin{gathered}
1 < t < 3 \;\text{(seconds)}
\end{gathered}\]
The particle is more than 15 metres above the ground between 1 and 3 seconds after launch.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle 1 < t < 3\) seconds
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 48\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-048)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 48 (ise4-048)}
\textit{Difficulty: Challenge \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{A company's daily profit (in pounds) from selling \( n \) units of a product is modelled by \( P(n) = -n^2 + 80n - 700 \). Find the values of \( n \) (with \( n \geq 0 \)) for which the daily profit is at least \pounds 900.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Translate "at least \pounds 900" into an inequality.}
\[\begin{gathered}
-n^2 + 80n - 700 \geq 900
\end{gathered}\]
"At least" allows equality, so we use a non-strict \( \geq \).

\textit{Step 2: Rearrange to standard form.}
\[\begin{gathered}
-n^2 + 80n - 1600 \geq 0 \;\Longleftrightarrow\; n^2 - 80n + 1600 \leq 0
\end{gathered}\]
Subtract 900 from both sides, then multiply by \( -1 \) and reverse the inequality.

\textit{Step 3: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
n^2 - 80n + 1600 = (n - 40)^2
\end{gathered}\]
Discriminant \( = 80^2 - 4(1)(1600) = 6400 - 6400 = 0 \) --- a repeated root at \( n = 40 \).

\textit{Step 4: Solve \( (n - 40)^2 \leq 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
(n - 40)^2 \geq 0 \text{ always, with equality only at } n = 40
\end{gathered}\]
A real square cannot be negative, so \( (n - 40)^2 \leq 0 \) forces \( (n - 40)^2 = 0 \), giving \( n = 40 \).

\textit{Step 5: Verify the physical domain.}
\[\begin{gathered}
n = 40 \geq 0 \;\checkmark
\end{gathered}\]
Selling 40 units yields exactly \pounds 900 profit, the maximum possible from this model. No other sales level achieves \pounds 900 or more.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle n = 40\) units
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 49\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-049)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 49 (ise4-049)}
\textit{Difficulty: Challenge \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{The line \( y = x + 1 \) and the curve \( y = x^2 - 5 \) are sketched on the same axes. Find the set of values of \( x \) for which the line lies strictly above the curve.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Translate the geometric condition into an inequality.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x + 1 > x^2 - 5
\end{gathered}\]
The line is strictly above the curve where its \( y \)-value is strictly greater than the curve's \( y \)-value.

\textit{Step 2: Rearrange to standard form.}
\[\begin{gathered}
0 > x^2 - x - 6 \;\Longleftrightarrow\; x^2 - x - 6 < 0
\end{gathered}\]
Bring all terms to one side; the \( x^2 \) coefficient is positive (we want it that way).

\textit{Step 3: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - x - 6 = (x - 3)(x + 2)
\end{gathered}\]
Roots \( x = -2 \) and \( x = 3 \), which are the \( x \)-coordinates of the intersection points of the line and the curve.

\textit{Step 4: Apply the U-shape rule.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x - 3)(x + 2) < 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; -2 < x < 3
\end{gathered}\]
Between the two intersection points the line sits above the curve; outside them the curve overtakes the line.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle -2 < x < 3\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Quadratic Inequalities 50\quad\small\texttt{(ise4-050)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quadratic Inequalities 50 (ise4-050)}
\textit{Difficulty: Challenge \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 7}

\question{The curve \( C \) has equation \( y = x^2 - 6x + 11 \) and the line \( \ell \) has equation \( y = 2x - 4 \). (a) Show that the curve lies entirely above the \( x \)-axis. (b) Find the set of values of \( x \) for which the curve lies on or below the line.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Show \( x^2 - 6x + 11 > 0 \) for all real \( x \) using the discriminant.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\Delta = (-6)^2 - 4(1)(11) = 36 - 44 = -8 < 0
\end{gathered}\]
The discriminant is negative, so the quadratic has no real roots and the parabola never crosses the x-axis.

\textit{Step 2: Combine with the sign of \( a \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
a = 1 > 0, \; \Delta < 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; x^2 - 6x + 11 > 0 \text{ for all } x
\end{gathered}\]
When \( \Delta < 0 \) the parabola is entirely on one side of the x-axis, taking the sign of \( a \). Since \( a > 0 \), it is strictly above. (Alternatively, completing the square gives \( (x - 3)^2 + 2 \geq 2 > 0 \) for all \( x \).)

\textit{Step 3: (b) Translate "curve on or below line" into an inequality.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 6x + 11 \leq 2x - 4
\end{gathered}\]
The curve's \( y \)-value is at most the line's \( y \)-value.

\textit{Step 4: Rearrange to standard form.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 8x + 15 \leq 0
\end{gathered}\]
Subtract \( 2x - 4 \) from both sides and tidy.

\textit{Step 5: Factorise and solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x - 3)(x - 5) \leq 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; 3 \leq x \leq 5
\end{gathered}\]
U-shape, non-positive on the closed interval between the roots. The endpoints \( x = 3 \) and \( x = 5 \) are the \( x \)-coordinates of the intersection points of \( C \) and \( \ell \), and are included because of \( \leq \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) Shown: \(\displaystyle \Delta < 0\) and \(\displaystyle a > 0\), so the quadratic is positive for all \(\displaystyle x\). (b) \(\displaystyle 3 \leq x \leq 5\)
\end{answerbox}
